In September 2013, Miyazaki announced he was retiring from the production of feature films due to his age, but wished to continue working on the displays at the Ghibli Museum.[^363] [^364] Miyazaki was awarded the [Academy Honorary Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Honorary_Award "Academy Honorary Award") at the [Governors Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governors_Awards "Governors Awards") in November 2014.[^365] He developed *[Boro the Caterpillar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boro_the_Caterpillar "Boro the Caterpillar")*, an animated short film which was first discussed during pre-production for *Princess Mononoke*.[^366] It was screened exclusively at the Ghibli Museum in July 2017.[^367] Around this time, Miyazaki was working on a manga titled *Teppo Samurai*.[^368] In February 2019, a four-part documentary was broadcast on the [NHK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NHK "NHK") network titled *10 Years with Hayao Miyazaki*, documenting production of his films in his private studio.[^369] In 2019, Miyazaki approved a musical adaptation of *Nausicaä of the Valley of the Wind*, as it was performed by a [kabuki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabuki "Kabuki") troupe.[^370]

In August 2016, Miyazaki proposed a new feature-length film, *Kimi-tachi wa Dō Ikiru ka* (titled *[The Boy and the Heron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Boy_and_the_Heron "The Boy and the Heron")* in English), on which he began animation work without receiving official approval.[^367] The film opened in Japanese theaters on July 14, 2023.[^371] It was preceded by a minimal marketing campaign, forgoing trailers, commercials, and advertisements, a response from Suzuki to his perceived oversaturation of marketing materials in mainstream films.[^372] It was commercially successful, grossing US$294.2 million worldwide,[^373] making it one of the highest-grossing anime films in several countries and one of the highest-grossing Japanese films ever.[^374] [^375] [^376] The film's production was documented in Kaku Arakawa's *[Hayao Miyazaki and the Heron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hayao_Miyazaki_and_the_Heron "Hayao Miyazaki and the Heron")*.[^377]

*The Boy and the Heron* won Miyazaki his second Academy Award for Best Animated Feature at the [96th Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/96th_Academy_Awards "96th Academy Awards"),[^378] becoming the oldest director to do so and [the first two-time Japanese winner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Academy_Award_winners_and_nominees_from_Japan "List of Academy Award winners and nominees from Japan") in the category (and second overall); Miyazaki did not attend the show due to his advanced age.[^379] Despite claims that *The Boy and the Heron* would be Miyazaki's final film, Studio Ghibli vice president Junichi Nishioka said in September 2023 that Miyazaki continued to attend the office daily to plan his next film.[^380] Suzuki said he could no longer convince Miyazaki to retire.[^381] Studio Ghibli became a subsidiary of [Nippon Television Holdings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nippon_Television_Holdings "Nippon Television Holdings") in October 2023, with Miyazaki as its honorary chairman.[^382]

## Views

> "If you don't spend time watching real people, you can't do this, because you've never seen it. Some people spend their lives interested only in themselves. Almost all Japanese animation is produced with hardly any basis taken from observing real people... It's produced by humans who can't stand looking at other humans. And that's why the industry is full of *[otaku](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otaku "Otaku")*!"

Hayao Miyazaki, January 2014 [^383]

Miyazaki has often criticized the state of the animation industry, stating that some animators lack a foundational understanding of their subjects and do not prioritize realism.[^384] He is particularly critical of Japanese animation, saying that anime is "produced by humans who can't stand looking at other humans... that's why the industry is full of *otaku*!" [^383] He has frequently criticized *otaku*, including "fanatics" of guns and fighter aircraft, declaring it a "fetish" and refusing to identify himself as such.[^385] [^386] He bemoaned the state of [Disney animated films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disney_animated_films "Disney animated films") in 1988, saying "they show nothing but contempt for the audience".[^387]

Miyazaki has criticized the use of [artificial intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artificial_intelligence "Artificial intelligence") (AI) in animation. When shown an animated [zombie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zombie "Zombie") generated by artificial intelligence in 2016, Miyazaki was reminded of his friend with a disability and felt the animator "has no idea what pain is whatsoever"; he was "utterly disgusted" by the work, which he called "an insult to life itself", with no plans to use artificial intelligence at Studio Ghibli.[^388] [^389] Miyazaki's comments resurfaced in March 2025 after the [generative artificial intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generative_artificial_intelligence "Generative artificial intelligence") model [ChatGPT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ChatGPT "ChatGPT") was updated to produce works strongly resembling artists' styles, including Studio Ghibli's. Many criticized the update, citing Miyazaki's comments;[^390] [^391] [^392] actress and director [Zelda Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zelda_Williams "Zelda Williams") wrote "fuck AI", noting Miyazaki would "absolutely despise the [technological piracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artificial_intelligence_and_copyright "Artificial intelligence and copyright") and [negative effects on our environment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_impact_of_artificial_intelligence "Environmental impact of artificial intelligence") ".[^393]

Miyazaki considers himself "traditionally... a [leftist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leftist "Leftist") in terms of emotional affinity", emphasizing his commitment to his ideals despite changing political and economic landscapes.[^394] He abandoned his [Marxist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marxist "Marxist") values while creating *Nausicaä of the Valley of the Wind* as he felt Nausicaä's status in an elite class did not affect her [class consciousness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Class_consciousness "Class consciousness"), recognizing that "No matter what class people are born into, idiots are still idiots and good people are still good".[^395] After the release of *The Wind Rises* in 2013, Miyazaki recognized leftist values in his movies, citing his influence by [communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism "Communism") as defined by [Karl Marx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Marx "Karl Marx"), but criticized real experiments of [socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism "Socialism") in countries like [the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism_in_the_Soviet_Union "Socialism in the Soviet Union").[^396]

Miyazaki felt events like [Japan's ownership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kwantung_Leased_Territory "Kwantung Leased Territory") of the [Liaodong Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liaodong_Peninsula "Liaodong Peninsula") and its [invasion of Manchuria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_invasion_of_Manchuria "Japanese invasion of Manchuria") led him to lack an affinity for his homeland, though in his thirties he examined that the land itself had "tremendous power".[^397] In 2013, he criticized [Japanese Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_Prime_Minister "Japanese Prime Minister") [Shinzo Abe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shinzo_Abe "Shinzo Abe") 's proposed Constitutional amendment that would allow Abe to revise the clause outlawing war as a means to settle international disputes.[^12] Miyazaki felt Abe wished to "leave his name in history as a great man who revised the Constitution and its interpretation", describing it as "despicable" [^399] and stating "People who don't think enough shouldn't meddle with the constitution".[^400] In 2015, Miyazaki disapproved Abe's denial of Japan's military aggression, stating Japan "should clearly say that \[they\] inflicted enormous damage on China and express deep remorse over it".[^399] He felt the government should give a "proper apology" to Korean [comfort women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comfort_women "Comfort women") who were forced to service the Japanese army during World War II and suggested the [Senkaku Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senkaku_Islands "Senkaku Islands") be "split in half" or controlled by both China and Japan.[^232] In 2024, Miyazaki acknowledged the "terrible things" Japan committed against the Philippines [during the war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_occupation_of_the_Philippines "Japanese occupation of the Philippines").[^401]

When *Spirited Away* was nominated at the 75th Academy Awards in 2003, Miyazaki refused to attend in protest of the United States's involvement in the Iraq War, and later said he "didn't want to visit a country that was bombing Iraq".[^402] He did not publicly express this opinion at the request of his producer until 2009 when he lifted his boycott and attended [San Diego Comic-Con](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Diego_Comic-Con "San Diego Comic-Con") as a favor to his friend John Lasseter.[^402] Miyazaki also expressed his opinion about [the terrorist attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlie_Hebdo_shooting "Charlie Hebdo shooting") at the offices of the French [satirical magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satirical_magazine "Satirical magazine") *[Charlie Hebdo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlie_Hebdo "Charlie Hebdo")*, criticizing the magazine's decision to publish the content cited as the catalyst for the incident; he felt caricatures should be made of politicians, not cultures.[^403] In November 2016, Miyazaki believed "many of the people who voted for [Brexit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brexit "Brexit") and [Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_2016_presidential_campaign "Donald Trump 2016 presidential campaign") " were affected by the increase in unemployment due to companies "building cars in Mexico because of low wages and \[selling\] them in the US".[^404] He did not think Donald Trump would be [elected president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election "2016 United States presidential election"), calling it "a terrible thing", but said Trump's political opponent [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton "Hillary Clinton") was "terrible as well".[^404]

## Themes

Miyazaki's works are characterized by the recurrence of themes such as feminism,[^405] [^406] [^407] [environmentalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmentalism "Environmentalism"), [pacifism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacifism "Pacifism"),[^408] [^409] [^410] love, and family.[^411] [^412] [^413] His narratives are also notable for not pitting a hero against an unsympathetic antagonist;[^414] [^415] [^416] Miyazaki felt *Spirited Away* ' s Chihiro "manages not because she has destroyed the 'evil', but because she has acquired the ability to survive".[^417]

Miyazaki's films often emphasize environmentalism and the Earth's fragility.[^418] [Margaret Talbot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaret_Talbot "Margaret Talbot") stated Miyazaki dislikes modern technology, and believes much of modern culture is "thin and shallow and fake"; he anticipates a time with "no more high-rises".[^419] Miyazaki felt frustrated growing up in the [Shōwa period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sh%C5%8Dwa_period "Shōwa period") from 1955 to 1965 because "nature—the mountains and rivers—was being destroyed in the name of economic progress".[^420] Peter Schellhase of *[The Imaginative Conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Imaginative_Conservative "The Imaginative Conservative")* identified that several antagonists of Miyazaki's films "attempt to dominate nature in pursuit of political domination, and are ultimately destructive to both nature and human civilization".[^413] Miyazaki is critical of exploitation under both communism and [capitalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitalism "Capitalism"), as well as [globalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globalization "Globalization") and its effects on modern life, believing "a company is common property of the people that work there".[^421] Ram Prakash Dwivedi identified values of [Mahatma Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahatma_Gandhi "Mahatma Gandhi") in the films of Miyazaki.[^422]

Several of Miyazaki's films feature anti-war themes. Daisuke Akimoto of *Animation Studies* categorized *Porco Rosso* as "anti-war propaganda" and felt the protagonist, Porco, transforms into a pig partly due to his extreme distaste of militarism.[^13] Akimoto also argues that *The Wind Rises* reflects Miyazaki's "antiwar pacifism", despite Miyazaki stating that the film does not attempt to "denounce" war.[^423] Schellhase also identifies *Princess Mononoke* as a pacifist film due to the protagonist, Ashitaka; instead of joining the campaign of revenge against humankind, as his ethnic history would lead him to do, Ashitaka strives for peace.[^413] David Loy and Linda Goodhew argue both *Nausicaä of the Valley of the Wind* and *Princess Mononoke* do not depict traditional evil, but the [Buddhist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist "Buddhist") roots of evil: greed, ill will, and delusion; according to Buddhism, the roots of evil must transform into "generosity, loving-kindness and wisdom" in order to overcome suffering, and both Nausicaä and Ashitaka accomplish this.[^424] When characters in Miyazaki's films are forced to engage in violence, it is shown as being a difficult task; in *Howl's Moving Castle*, Howl is forced to fight an inescapable battle in defense of those he loves, and it almost destroys him, though he is ultimately saved by Sophie's love and bravery.[^413]

Suzuki described Miyazaki as a feminist in reference to his attitude to female workers.[^425] Miyazaki has described his female characters as "brave, self-sufficient girls that don't think twice about fighting for what they believe in with all their heart", stating they may "need a friend, or a supporter, but never a saviour" and "any woman is just as capable of being a hero as any man".[^426] *Nausicaä of the Valley of the Wind* was lauded for its positive portrayal of women, particularly protagonist Nausicaä.[^159] [^161] Schellhase noted the female characters in Miyazaki's films are not objectified or sexualized, and possess complex and individual characteristics absent from Hollywood productions.[^413] Schellhase also identified a "coming of age" element for the heroines in Miyazaki's films, as they each discover "individual personality and strengths".[^413] Gabrielle Bellot of *[The Atlantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Atlantic "The Atlantic")* wrote that, in his films, Miyazaki "shows a keen understanding of the complexities of what it might mean to be a woman". In particular, Bellot cites *Nausicaä of the Valley of the Wind*, praising the film's challenging of gender expectations, and the strong and independent nature of Nausicaä. Bellot also noted *Princess Mononoke* ' s San represents the "conflict between selfhood and expression".[^427]

Miyazaki is concerned with the sense of wonder in young people, seeking to maintain themes of love and family in his films.[^413] Michael Toscano of *Curator* found Miyazaki "fears Japanese children are dimmed by a culture of overconsumption, overprotection, utilitarian education, careerism, techno-industrialism, and a secularism that is swallowing Japan's native animism".[^428] Schellhase wrote that several of Miyazaki's works feature themes of love and romance, but felt emphasis is placed on "the way lonely and vulnerable individuals are integrated into relationships of mutual reliance and responsibility, which generally benefit everyone around them".[^413] He also found many of the protagonists in Miyazaki's films present an idealized image of families, whereas others are dysfunctional.[^413]

Miyazaki forgoes traditional screenplays in his productions, instead developing the narrative as he designs the storyboards, stating "We never know where the story will go but we just keep working on the film as it develops".[^429] Miyazaki has employed traditional animation methods in all of his films, drawing each frame by hand; computer-generated imagery has been employed in several of his later films, beginning with *Princess Mononoke*, to "enrich the visual look",[^430] though he ensures each film can "retain the right ratio between working by hand and computer... and still be able to call my films 2D".[^431] He oversees every frame of his films.[^432] For character designs, Miyazaki draws original drafts used by animation directors to create reference sheets, which Miyazaki then corrects in his style.[^433] He prioritizes logical plots even for child-friendly contents.[^434]

Miyazaki has cited several Japanese artists as his influences, including [Sanpei Shirato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanpei_Shirato "Sanpei Shirato"),[^39] Osamu Tezuka, Soji Yamakawa,[^41] and Isao Takahata,[^435] and Western artists and animators like [Frédéric Back](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fr%C3%A9d%C3%A9ric_Back "Frédéric Back"),[^429] Jean Giraud,[^139] Paul Grimault,[^429] [Yuri Norstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuri_Norstein "Yuri Norstein"),[^436] and animation studio [Aardman Animations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aardman_Animations "Aardman Animations") (specifically the works of [Nick Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nick_Park "Nick Park")).[^437] [^14] Several authors have also influenced his works, including [Lewis Carroll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lewis_Carroll "Lewis Carroll"),[^431] [Roald Dahl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roald_Dahl "Roald Dahl"),[^439] Ursula K. Le Guin,[^146] [Philippa Pearce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippa_Pearce "Philippa Pearce"), [Rosemary Sutcliff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosemary_Sutcliff "Rosemary Sutcliff"), and [Antoine de Saint-Exupéry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antoine_de_Saint-Exup%C3%A9ry "Antoine de Saint-Exupéry"),[^49] Specific works that have influenced Miyazaki include *[Animal Farm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_Farm "Animal Farm")* (1945),[^431] *The Snow Queen* (1957),[^429] and *[The King and the Mockingbird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_King_and_the_Mockingbird "The King and the Mockingbird")* (1980);[^431] *The Snow Queen* is said to be the true catalyst for Miyazaki's filmography, influencing his training and work.[^440] When animating young children, Miyazaki often takes inspiration from his friends' children and memories of his own childhood.[^441]

## Personal life

![[~/×/b7570a8ec2151c7a8062b6b252fea137_MD5.jpg]]

Miyazaki's son, director Goro Miyazaki

Miyazaki's wife, Akemi Ōta (大田朱美), was born in 1938 and hired as an inbetween artist at Toei Animation in 1958, working on *Panda and the Magic Serpent* and *[Alakazam the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alakazam_the_Great "Alakazam the Great")* (1960).[^442] She and Miyazaki met at Toei in 1964,[^442] [^18] and they married in October 1965.[^52] At Toei, they worked together on *The Great Adventure of Horus, Prince of the Sun* and *The Wonderful World of Puss 'n Boots*.[^442] They have two sons: Goro, born in January 1967, and Keisuke, born in April 1969.[^69] Becoming a father changed Miyazaki and he tried to produce work to please his children.[^68]

Miyazaki initially fulfilled a promise to his wife that they would both continue to work after Goro's birth, dropping him off at [preschool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preschool "Preschool") for the day; however, upon seeing Goro's exhaustion walking home one day, Miyazaki decided they could not continue, and his wife quit in 1972 to stay at home and raise their children.[^443] [^442] She was reluctant to do so but considered it necessary to allow Miyazaki to focus on his work.[^442] Miyazaki's dedication to his work harmed his relationship with his children as he was often absent. Goro watched his father's works to "understand" him since the two rarely talked.[^444] Miyazaki said he "tried to be a good father, but in the end \[he\] wasn't a very good parent",[^443] and later said he felt he owed "that little boy an apology".[^27] In 2006, Goro said his father "gets zero marks as a father, but full marks as a director of animated films".[^444] [^15]

Goro worked at a landscape design firm before beginning to work at the Ghibli Museum;[^18] [^443] he designed the garden on its rooftop and eventually became its curator.[^18] [^68] Keisuke studied forestry at [Shinshu University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shinshu_University "Shinshu University") and works as a wood artist;[^18] [^443] [^445] he designed a woodcut print that appears in *Whisper of the Heart*.[^445] Miyazaki's niece, Mei Okuyama, who was the inspiration behind the character Mei in *My Neighbor Totoro*, is married to animation artist [Daisuke Tsutsumi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daisuke_Tsutsumi "Daisuke Tsutsumi").[^446]

## Legacy

![[~/×/cfeec9b3a49383cd726060c02dd3fee7_MD5.jpg]]

Wall mural depicting several anime characters

![[~/×/a3d27bee2c08e3654ec2c1bde84d29a1_MD5.jpg]]

Wall mural depicting several anime characters

Murals honoring Miyazaki's works in [Aguascalientes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aguascalientes "Aguascalientes"), Mexico (top), and [Limoges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limoges "Limoges"), France (bottom)

Miyazaki was described as the "godfather of animation in Japan" by [BBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC "BBC") 's Tessa Wong in 2016, citing his craftsmanship and humanity, the themes of his films, and his inspiration to younger artists.[^447] Courtney Lanning of *[Arkansas Democrat-Gazette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arkansas_Democrat-Gazette "Arkansas Democrat-Gazette")* named him one of the world's greatest animators, comparing him to Osamu Tezuka and [Walt Disney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Disney "Walt Disney");[^448] Miyazaki has been called "the Disney of Japan", though Helen McCarthy considered comparison to [Akira Kurosawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akira_Kurosawa "Akira Kurosawa") more appropriate due to the combination of grandeur and sensitivity in his work, dubbing him "the Kurosawa of animation".[^449]

Swapnil Dhruv Bose of *Far Out Magazine* wrote that Miyazaki's work "has shaped not only the future of animation but also filmmaking in general", and that it helped "generation after generation of young viewers to observe the magic that exists in the mundane".[^450] Richard James Havis of *[South China Morning Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_China_Morning_Post "South China Morning Post")* called him a "genius... who sets exacting standards for himself, his peers and studio staff".[^451] *[Paste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paste_\(magazine\) "Paste (magazine)")* ' s Toussaint Egan described Miyazaki as "one of anime's great auteurs", whose "stories of such singular thematic vision and unmistakable aesthetic" captured viewers otherwise unfamiliar with anime.[^452] Miyazaki became the subject of an exhibit at the [Academy Museum of Motion Pictures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Museum_of_Motion_Pictures "Academy Museum of Motion Pictures") in Los Angeles in 2021, featuring over 400 objects from his films.[^453]

Miyazaki has frequently been cited as an inspiration to numerous animators, directors and writers around the world, including [Wes Anderson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wes_Anderson "Wes Anderson"),[^454] [Tony Bancroft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Bancroft "Tony Bancroft"),[^254] [James Cameron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Cameron "James Cameron"),[^455] [Barry Cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barry_Cook "Barry Cook"),[^254] [Dean DeBlois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dean_DeBlois "Dean DeBlois"),[^456] [Guillermo del Toro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guillermo_del_Toro "Guillermo del Toro"),[^457] [Pete Docter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pete_Docter "Pete Docter"),[^458] Mamoru Hosoda,[^459] [Bong Joon Ho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bong_Joon_Ho "Bong Joon Ho"),[^460] [Travis Knight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Travis_Knight "Travis Knight"),[^461] John Lasseter,[^462] Nick Park,[^463] [Henry Selick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Selick "Henry Selick"),[^464] [Makoto Shinkai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makoto_Shinkai "Makoto Shinkai"),[^465] [Steven Spielberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steven_Spielberg "Steven Spielberg"),[^466] and [Gints Zilbalodis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gints_Zilbalodis "Gints Zilbalodis").[^467] [Glen Keane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glen_Keane "Glen Keane") said Miyazaki is a "huge influence" on [Walt Disney Animation Studios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Disney_Animation_Studios "Walt Disney Animation Studios") and has been "part of our heritage" ever since *[The Rescuers Down Under](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Rescuers_Down_Under "The Rescuers Down Under")* (1990).[^468] The [Disney Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disney_Renaissance "Disney Renaissance") era was also prompted by competition with the development of Miyazaki's films.[^469] Artists from [Pixar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pixar "Pixar") and [Aardman Studios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aardman_Studios "Aardman Studios") signed a tribute stating, "You're our inspiration, Miyazaki-san!" [^463] He has also been cited as inspiration for video game designers including [Shigeru Miyamoto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shigeru_Miyamoto "Shigeru Miyamoto") on *[The Legend of Zelda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Legend_of_Zelda "The Legend of Zelda")* [^470] and [Hironobu Sakaguchi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hironobu_Sakaguchi "Hironobu Sakaguchi") on *[Final Fantasy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Final_Fantasy "Final Fantasy")*,[^471] as well as the television series *[Avatar: The Last Airbender](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avatar:_The_Last_Airbender "Avatar: The Last Airbender")*,[^472] and the video game *[Ori and the Blind Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ori_and_the_Blind_Forest "Ori and the Blind Forest")* (2015).[^473]

[Several books have been written](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hayao_Miyazaki_bibliography "Hayao Miyazaki bibliography") about Miyazaki by scholars such as [Raz Greenberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raz_Greenberg "Raz Greenberg"), Helen McCarthy, and [Susan J. Napier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susan_J._Napier "Susan J. Napier");[^474] according to Jeff Lenburg, more papers have been written about Miyazaki than any other Japanese artist.[^475] Studio Ghibli has searched for some time for Miyazaki and Suzuki's successor to lead the studio;[^476] Kondō, the director of *Whisper of the Heart*, was initially considered, but died from a sudden heart attack in 1998.[^477] Some candidates were considered by 2023—including Miyazaki's son Goro, who declined—but the studio was not able to find a successor.[^478]

Miyazaki won the [Ōfuji Noburō Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%8Cfuji_Nobur%C5%8D_Award "Ōfuji Noburō Award") at the [Mainichi Film Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainichi_Film_Awards "Mainichi Film Awards") for *The Castle of Cagliostro* (1979), *Nausicaä of the Valley of the Wind* (1984), *Laputa: Castle in the Sky* (1986),[^479] *My Neighbor Totoro* (1988),[^480] and *The Boy and the Heron* (2023),[^481] and the [Mainichi Film Award for Best Animation Film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainichi_Film_Award_for_Best_Animation_Film "Mainichi Film Award for Best Animation Film") for *Kiki's Delivery Service* (1989),[^480] *Porco Rosso* (1992),[^482] *Princess Mononoke* (1997), *Spirited Away* (2001),[^480] and *Whale Hunt* (2001).[^482] *Spirited Away* and *The Boy and the Heron* were awarded the Academy Award for Best Animated Feature,[^286] [^378] while *Howl's Moving Castle* (2004) and *The Wind Rises* (2013) received nominations.[^314] [^360] He was named a [Person of Cultural Merit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Person_of_Cultural_Merit "Person of Cultural Merit") by the Japanese government in November 2012, for outstanding cultural contributions.[^483] *[Time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_\(magazine\) "Time (magazine)")* named him one of the [100 most influential people in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_100 "Time 100") in 2005 and 2024,[^318] [^484] and [Gold House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gold_House "Gold House") honored him on its [Most Impactful Asians A100 list](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gold_House#A100 "Gold House") in 2024.[^485] He was an honoree of the [Ramon Magsaysay Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramon_Magsaysay_Award "Ramon Magsaysay Award") in 2024 for his usage of art to "illuminate the human condition".[^486] Miyazaki's other accolades include several [Annie Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annie_Awards "Annie Awards"),[^487] [^488] [^489] [Japan Academy Film Prizes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan_Academy_Film_Prize "Japan Academy Film Prize"),[^490] [^491] [^359] [Kinema Junpo Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kinema_Junpo_Awards "Kinema Junpo Awards"),[^492] [^493] and [Tokyo Anime Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokyo_Anime_Awards "Tokyo Anime Awards").[^348] [^494] [^495]

### Journal articles

- Akimoto, Daisuke (September 2, 2013). "Miyazaki's new animated film and its antiwar pacifism: *The Wind Rises* (*Kaze Tachinu*) ". *Ritsumeikan Journal of Asia Pacific Studies*. **32**: 165– 167.
- Akimoto, Daisuke (October 1, 2014). Ratelle, Amy (ed.). ["A Pig, the State, and War: *Porco Rosso* (*Kurenai no Buta*) "](https://journal.animationstudies.org/daisuke-akimoto-a-pig-the-state-and-war-porco-rosso/). *Animation Studies*. **9**. [Society for Animation Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Society_for_Animation_Studies "Society for Animation Studies"). [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20180125135022/https://journal.animationstudies.org/daisuke-akimoto-a-pig-the-state-and-war-porco-rosso/) from the original on January 25, 2018. Retrieved March 18, 2017.
- ナウシカの道 連載 1 宮崎駿・マンガの系譜 \[The Road to *Nausicaä*, episode 1, Hayao Miyazaki's Manga Genealogy\]. *[Animage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animage "Animage")* (in Japanese) (61). Tokyo: [Tokuma Shoten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokuma_Shoten "Tokuma Shoten"): 172– 173. June 10, 1983.
- 宮崎駿Xピーター・ロードXデイビッド・スプロスクトンat三鷹の森ジブリ美術館 \[Hayao Miyazaki x Peter Lord x David Sprockett at the Ghibli Museum in Mitaka\]. *[Animage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animage "Animage")* ( Lasseter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Lasseter "John Lasseter") wanted Miyazaki to travel to the United States to work on the translated version, but Miyazaki trusted Lasseter to handle it.[^291] *Spirited Away* ' s hopping lamp character is seen as an homage to Lasseter's character [Luxo Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luxo_Jr._\(character\) "Luxo Jr. (character)")[^292] The film's successful American release through Buena Vista cemented Studio Ghibli's reputation in Western regions [^293] [^294] [^295] and established Miyazaki's popularity in North America;[^296] it was the first animated film to win the [Golden Bear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Bear "Golden Bear") at the [Berlin International Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/52nd_Berlin_International_Film_Festival "52nd Berlin International Film Festival") (tied with *[Bloody Sunday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bloody_Sunday_\(film\) "Bloody Sunday (film)")*) [^291] and the first Japanese film to win the [Best Animated Feature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Animated_Feature "Academy Award for Best Animated Feature") at the [Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/75th_Academy_Awards "75th Academy Awards"),[^293] alongside several other accolades.[^297] It has been frequently ranked among the greatest films of the 21st century.[^298] [^299] [^300] Upon completing the film, like with *Princess Mononoke*, Miyazaki declared it his last.[^301] He traveled to France in December 2001 and the United States in September 2002 to promote the film.[^302] Following the death of Tokuma in September 2000, Miyazaki served as the head of his funeral committee.[^270] Miyazaki wrote and directed more short films for the Ghibli Museum: *[Koro's Big Day Out](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koro%27s_Big_Day_Out "Koro's Big Day Out")*, which screened from January 2002, and *[Mei and the Kittenbus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mei_and_the_Kittenbus "Mei and the Kittenbus")*, which screened from October.[^303] One of the short films, *[Imaginary Flying Machines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imaginary_Flying_Machines "Imaginary Flying Machines")*, was later screened as [in-flight entertainment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/In-flight_entertainment "In-flight entertainment") by Japan Airlines alongside *Porco Rosso*.[^304]

![[~/×/e0d28dbb9b4f97dd66f4331075cad9f9_MD5.jpg]]

Miyazaki created several short films for the Ghibli Museum in between his feature films. 303

Studio Ghibli announced the production of *[Howl's Moving Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howl%27s_Moving_Castle_\(film\) "Howl's Moving Castle (film)")* in September 2001, based on [the novel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howl%27s_Moving_Castle_\(novel\) "Howl's Moving Castle (novel)") by [Diana Wynne Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diana_Wynne_Jones "Diana Wynne Jones"),[^305] which Miyazaki had read in 1999.[^306] Toei Animation's [Mamoru Hosoda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamoru_Hosoda "Mamoru Hosoda") was originally selected to direct the film, but disagreements between Hosoda and Studio Ghibli executives led to the project's abandonment in 2002.[^307] [^308] After six months, Studio Ghibli resurrected the project. Miyazaki was inspired to direct the film, struck by the image of a castle moving around the countryside, and he took creative liberties in its depiction.[^18] Some computer animation was used to animate the castle's movements,[^309] though Miyazaki dictated it consist of no more than 10 percent of the film.[^308] Miyazaki traveled to [Colmar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colmar "Colmar") and [Riquewihr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riquewihr "Riquewihr") in [Alsace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alsace "Alsace"), France, to study the architecture and the surroundings for the film's setting,[^310] while additional inspiration came from the concepts of future technology in [Albert Robida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Robida "Albert Robida") 's work.[^311] The war featured in the film was thematically influenced by [the 2003 invasion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_invasion_of_Iraq "2003 invasion of Iraq") and subsequent [war in Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_War "Iraq War"), the events of which enraged Miyazaki.[^312]

*Howl's Moving Castle* was released on November 20, 2004, and received widespread critical acclaim. The film received the [Golden Osella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Osella "Golden Osella") for Technical Excellence at the [61st Venice International Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/61st_Venice_International_Film_Festival "61st Venice International Film Festival"),[^313] and was nominated for the Academy Award for Best Animated Feature.[^314] In Japan, the film sold more than 1.1 million tickets within two days [^315] and grossed a record US$14.5 million in its first week.[^18] It became Japan's third-highest-grossing film,[^315] and remains among the top rankings with a worldwide gross of over ¥19.3 billion.[^316] Miyazaki received the honorary Golden Lion for Lifetime Achievement award at the [62nd Venice International Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/62nd_Venice_International_Film_Festival "62nd Venice International Film Festival") in 2005.[^317] He visited the United States in June 2005 to promote the film.[^318]

In March 2005, Studio Ghibli split from Tokuma Shoten,[^319] and Miyazaki became corporate director.[^318] After *Howl's Moving Castle*, Miyazaki created some short films for the Ghibli Museum, for which he returned solely to traditional animation techniques;[^309] all three began screening in January 2006.[^320] Studio Ghibli obtained the rights to produce an adaptation of [Ursula K. Le Guin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ursula_K._Le_Guin "Ursula K. Le Guin") 's *[Earthsea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earthsea "Earthsea")* novels in 2003;[^321] Miyazaki had contacted her in the 1980s expressing interest but she declined, unaware of his work. Upon watching *My Neighbor Totoro* several years later, she expressed approval to the concept and met with Suzuki in August 2005, who wanted Miyazaki's son [Goro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goro_Miyazaki "Goro Miyazaki") to direct the film, as Miyazaki had wished to retire. Disappointed that Miyazaki was not directing but under the impression he would supervise his son's work, Le Guin approved of the film's production.[^322] Miyazaki later publicly opposed and criticized Goro's appointment as director.[^323] The film's designs were partly inspired by Miyazaki's manga *The Journey of Shuna*.[^324] During a screening of the film, Miyazaki commented, "You shouldn't make a picture based on your emotions".[^27] He later wrote a message for his son: "It was made honestly, so it was good".[^325]

In February 2006, Miyazaki traveled to the United Kingdom to research *A Trip to Tynemouth* (based on [Robert Westall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Westall "Robert Westall") 's "Blackham's Wimpy"), for which he designed the cover, created a short manga, and worked as editor;[^326] it was released in October.[^327] Miyazaki's next film, *[Ponyo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ponyo "Ponyo")*, began production in May 2006.[^328] It was initially inspired by " [The Little Mermaid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Little_Mermaid "The Little Mermaid") " by [Hans Christian Andersen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hans_Christian_Andersen "Hans Christian Andersen"), though began to take its own form as production continued.[^329] Miyazaki aimed for the film to celebrate the innocence and cheerfulness of a child's universe.[^328] He was intimately involved with the artwork, preferring to draw the sea and waves himself, as he enjoyed experimenting.[^330] Two short films— *[Looking for a Home](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Looking_for_a_Home_\(film\) "Looking for a Home (film)")* and *[Water Spider Monmon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mizugumo_Monmon "Mizugumo Monmon")* —were made for the Ghibli Museum shortly before *Ponyo* entered production as animation experiments for sea life.[^331]

*Ponyo* features 170,000 frames—a record for Miyazaki.[^332] Its seaside village was inspired by [Tomonoura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tomonoura "Tomonoura"), a town in [Setonaikai National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Setonaikai_National_Park "Setonaikai National Park"), where Miyazaki stayed in 2004.[^333] The main character, Sōsuke, is based on Gorō.[^334] Following its release on July 19, 2008, *Ponyo* was critically acclaimed, receiving Animation of the Year at the [32nd Japan Academy Film Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/32nd_Japan_Academy_Film_Prize "32nd Japan Academy Film Prize").[^335] The film was also a commercial success, earning ¥10 billion (US$93.2 million) in its first month [^334] and ¥15.5 billion by the end of 2008, placing it among the highest-grossing films in Japan;[^336] its box office earnings outpaced its ¥3.4 billion budget fivefold.[^337] In April 2008, Miyazaki founded Home of the Three Bears, a preschool for the children of Studio Ghibli employees for which he had worked on early architectural plans.[^338]

![[~/×/b81927a42f4b674f22309ac98e638885_MD5.jpg]]

Miyazaki's manga and film The Wind Rises tell the story of Mitsubishi A6M Zero fighter designer Jiro Horikoshi. 339

In early 2009, Miyazaki began writing a manga called *Kaze Tachinu* (風立ちぬ, *The Wind Rises*), telling the story of [Mitsubishi A6M Zero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitsubishi_A6M_Zero "Mitsubishi A6M Zero") fighter designer [Jiro Horikoshi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jiro_Horikoshi "Jiro Horikoshi"). The manga was first published in two issues of the Model Graphix magazine, published on February 25 and March 25, 2009.[^340] For the Ghibli Museum, Miyazaki wrote the short film *[A Sumo Wrestler's Tail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Sumo_Wrestler%27s_Tail "A Sumo Wrestler's Tail")*, directed by [Akihiko Yamashita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akihiko_Yamashita "Akihiko Yamashita"), and wrote and directed *[Mr. Dough and the Egg Princess](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mr._Dough_and_the_Egg_Princess "Mr. Dough and the Egg Princess")*; both started screening in 2010.[^341] From July 2008, Miyazaki planned and produced the film *[Arrietty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrietty "Arrietty")* (2010),[^342] for which he co-wrote the screenplay with [Keiko Niwa](https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E4%B8%B9%E7%BE%BD%E5%9C%AD%E5%AD%90 "ja:丹羽圭子"),[^343] based on the 1952 novel *[The Borrowers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Borrowers_\(novel\) "The Borrowers (novel)")*;[^344] it was the directorial debut of [Hiromasa Yonebayashi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiromasa_Yonebayashi "Hiromasa Yonebayashi"), who had started as an inbetween artist on *Princess Mononoke*.[^345] Miyazaki and Niwa wrote the screenplay for *[From Up on Poppy Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/From_Up_on_Poppy_Hill "From Up on Poppy Hill")* (2011), based the 1979–1980 manga *[Coquelicot-zaka kara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coquelicot-zaka_kara "Coquelicot-zaka kara")*; the film, directed by Goro Miyazaki, was the highest-grossing Japanese film in the country in 2011 and won [Animation of the Year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan_Academy_Film_Prize_for_Animation_of_the_Year "Japan Academy Film Prize for Animation of the Year") at the [Japan Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan_Academy_Awards "Japan Academy Awards").[^346] [^347]

Miyazaki wanted his next film to be a sequel to *Ponyo*, but Suzuki convinced him to instead adapt *Kaze Tachinu* to film.[^348] In November 2012, Studio Ghibli announced the production of *[The Wind Rises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Wind_Rises "The Wind Rises")*, based on *Kaze Tachinu*, to be released as a double bill alongside Takahata's *[The Tale of the Princess Kaguya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Tale_of_the_Princess_Kaguya_\(film\) "The Tale of the Princess Kaguya (film)")*;[^349] the latter was ultimately delayed.[^350] Miyazaki was inspired to create *The Wind Rises* after reading a quote from Horikoshi: "All I wanted to do was to make something beautiful".[^339] Several scenes in *The Wind Rises* were inspired by [Tatsuo Hori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tatsuo_Hori "Tatsuo Hori") 's novel *[The Wind Has Risen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Wind_Has_Risen "The Wind Has Risen")* (風立ちぬ), in which Hori wrote about his life experiences with his fiancée before she died from tuberculosis. The female lead character's name, Naoko Satomi, was borrowed from Hori's novel *Naoko* (菜穂子),[^351] while the name of a German man, Hans Castorp, taken from [Thomas Mann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Mann "Thomas Mann") 's *[The Magic Mountain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Magic_Mountain "The Magic Mountain")*.[^352] Naoko's struggles with tuberculosis echo the illness of Miyazaki's mother, and Horikoshi's story of growing from a young boy dreaming of airplanes to an inspirational artist is reflective of Miyazaki's own life.[^353]

*The Wind Rises* reflects Miyazaki's pacifist stance,[^339] continuing the themes of his earlier works, despite stating that condemning war was not the intention of the film;[^354] he felt that, despite his occupation, Horikoshi was not militant.[^355] Miyazaki was moved by the film, the first of his own works to make him cry.[^356] As Horikoshi, he cast [Hideaki Anno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hideaki_Anno "Hideaki Anno"), who had worked on *Nausicaä of the Valley of the Wind* and is known for creating *[Neon Genesis Evangelion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neon_Genesis_Evangelion_\(franchise\) "Neon Genesis Evangelion (franchise)")*.[^357] The film premiered on July 20, 2013,[^339] It received critical acclaim for its animation, narrative, and characters, though some viewers were critical of the film's focus on Horikoshi due to the impacts of his inventions and others were disappointed by its lack of fantastical elements.[^358] It was named Animation of the Year at the [37th Japan Academy Film Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/37th_Japan_Academy_Film_Prize "37th Japan Academy Film Prize") [^359] and was nominated for Best Animated Feature at the [86th Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/86th_Academy_Awards "86th Academy Awards").[^360] It was commercially successful, grossing ¥11.6 billion (US$110 million) at the Japanese box office, becoming the highest-grossing film in Japan in 2013.[^361] The film's production was documented in Mami Sunada's *[The Kingdom of Dreams and Madness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Kingdom_of_Dreams_and_Madness "The Kingdom of Dreams and Madness")*.[^357] [^362]

In September 2013, Miyazaki announced he was retiring from the production of feature films due to his age, but wished to continue working on the displays at the Ghibli Museum.[^363] [^364] Miyazaki was awarded the [Academy Honorary Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Honorary_Award "Academy Honorary Award") at the [Governors Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governors_Awards "Governors Awards") in November 2014.[^365] He developed *[Boro the Caterpillar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boro_the_Caterpillar "Boro the Caterpillar")*, an animated short film which was first discussed during pre-production for *Princess Mononoke*.[^366] It was screened exclusively at the Ghibli Museum in July 2017.[^367] Around this time, Miyazaki was working on a manga titled *Teppo Samurai*.[^368] In February 2019, a four-part documentary was broadcast on the [NHK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NHK "NHK") network titled *10 Years with Hayao Miyazaki*, documenting production of his films in his private studio.[^369] In 2019, Miyazaki approved a musical adaptation of *Nausicaä of the Valley of the Wind*, as it was performed by a [kabuki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabuki "Kabuki") troupe.[^370]

In August 2016, Miyazaki proposed a new feature-length film, *Kimi-tachi wa Dō Ikiru ka* (titled *[The Boy and the Heron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Boy_and_the_Heron "The Boy and the Heron")* in English), on which he began animation work without receiving official approval.[^367] The film opened in Japanese theaters on July 14, 2023.[^371] It was preceded by a minimal marketing campaign, forgoing trailers, commercials, and advertisements, a response from Suzuki to his perceived oversaturation of marketing materials in mainstream films.[^372] It was commercially successful, grossing US$294.2 million worldwide,[^373] making it one of the highest-grossing anime films in several countries and one of the highest-grossing Japanese films ever.[^374] [^375] [^376] The film's production was documented in Kaku Arakawa's *[Hayao Miyazaki and the Heron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hayao_Miyazaki_and_the_Heron "Hayao Miyazaki and the Heron")*.[^377]

*The Boy and the Heron* won Miyazaki his second Academy Award for Best Animated Feature at the [96th Academy Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/96th_Academy_Awards "96th Academy Awards"),[^378] becoming the oldest director to do so and [the first two-time Japanese winner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Academy_Award_winners_and_nominees_from_Japan "List of Academy Award winners and nominees from Japan") in the category (and second overall); Miyazaki did not attend the show due to his advanced age.[^379] Despite claims that *The Boy and the Heron* would be Miyazaki's final film, Studio Ghibli vice president Junichi Nishioka said in September 2023 that Miyazaki continued to attend the office daily to plan his next film.[^380] Suzuki said he could no longer convince Miyazaki to retire.[^381] Studio Ghibli became a subsidiary of [Nippon Television Holdings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nippon_Television_Holdings "Nippon Television Holdings") in October 2023, with Miyazaki as its honorary chairman.[^382]

## Views

> "If you don't spend time watching real people, you can't do this, because you've never seen it. Some people spend their lives interested only in themselves. Almost all Japanese animation is produced with hardly any basis taken from observing real people... It's produced by humans who can't stand looking at other humans. And that's why the industry is full of *[otaku](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otaku "Otaku")*!"

Hayao Miyazaki, January 2014 [^383]

Miyazaki has often criticized the state of the animation industry, stating that some animators lack a foundational understanding of their subjects and do not prioritize realism.[^384] He is particularly critical of Japanese animation, saying that anime is "produced by humans who can't stand looking at other humans... that's why the industry is full of *otaku*!" [^383] He has frequently criticized *otaku*, including "fanatics" of guns and fighter aircraft, declaring it a "fetish" and refusing to identify himself as such.[^385] [^386] He bemoaned the state of [Disney animated films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disney_animated_films "Disney animated films") in 1988, saying "they show nothing but contempt for the audience".[^387]

Miyazaki has criticized the use of [artificial intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artificial_intelligence "Artificial intelligence") (AI) in animation. When shown an animated [zombie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zombie "Zombie") generated by artificial intelligence in 2016, Miyazaki was reminded of his friend with a disability and felt the animator "has no idea what pain is whatsoever"; he was "utterly disgusted" by the work, which he called "an insult to life itself", with no plans to use artificial intelligence at Studio Ghibli.[^388] [^389] Miyazaki's comments resurfaced in March 2025 after the [generative artificial intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generative_artificial_intelligence "Generative artificial intelligence") model [ChatGPT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ChatGPT "ChatGPT") was updated to produce works strongly resembling artists' styles, including Studio Ghibli's. Many criticized the update, citing Miyazaki's comments;[^390] [^391] [^392] actress and director [Zelda Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zelda_Williams "Zelda Williams") wrote "fuck AI", noting Miyazaki would "absolutely despise the [technological piracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artificial_intelligence_and_copyright "Artificial intelligence and copyright") and [negative effects on our environment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_impact_of_artificial_intelligence "Environmental impact of artificial intelligence") ".[^393]

Miyazaki considers himself "traditionally... a [leftist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leftist "Leftist") in terms of emotional affinity", emphasizing his commitment to his ideals despite changing political and economic landscapes.[^394] He abandoned his [Marxist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marxist "Marxist") values while creating *Nausicaä of the Valley of the Wind* as he felt Nausicaä's status in an elite class did not affect her [class consciousness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Class_consciousness "Class consciousness"), recognizing that "No matter what class people are born into, idiots are still idiots and good people are still good".[^395] After the release of *The Wind Rises* in 2013, Miyazaki recognized leftist values in his movies, citing his influence by [communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism "Communism") as defined by [Karl Marx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Marx "Karl Marx"), but criticized real experiments of [socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism "Socialism") in countries like [the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism_in_the_Soviet_Union "Socialism in the Soviet Union").[^396]

Miyazaki felt events like [Japan's ownership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kwantung_Leased_Territory "Kwantung Leased Territory") of the [Liaodong Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liaodong_Peninsula "Liaodong Peninsula") and its [invasion of Manchuria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_invasion_of_Manchuria "Japanese invasion of Manchuria") led him to lack an affinity for his homeland, though in his thirties he examined that the land itself had "tremendous power".[^397] In 2013, he criticized [Japanese Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_Prime_Minister "Japanese Prime Minister") [Shinzo Abe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shinzo_Abe "Shinzo Abe") 's proposed Constitutional amendment that would allow Abe to revise the clause outlawing war as a means to settle international disputes.[^12] Miyazaki felt Abe wished to "leave his name in history as a great man who revised the Constitution and its interpretation", describing it as "despicable" [^399] and stating "People who don't think enough shouldn't meddle with the constitution".[^400] In 2015, Miyazaki disapproved Abe's denial of Japan's military aggression, stating Japan "should clearly say that \[they\] inflicted enormous damage on China and express deep remorse over it".[^399] He felt the government should give a "proper apology" to Korean [comfort women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comfort_women "Comfort women") who were forced to service the Japanese army during World War II and suggested the [Senkaku Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senkaku_Islands "Senkaku Islands") be "split in half" or controlled by both China and Japan.[^232] In 2024, Miyazaki acknowledged the "terrible things" Japan committed against the Philippines [during the war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_occupation_of_the_Philippines "Japanese occupation of the Philippines").[^401]

When *Spirited Away* was nominated at the 75th Academy Awards in 2003, Miyazaki refused to attend in protest of the United States's involvement in the Iraq War, and later said he "didn't want to visit a country that was bombing Iraq".[^402] He did not publicly express this opinion at the request of his producer until 2009 when he lifted his boycott and attended [San Diego Comic-Con](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Diego_Comic-Con "San Diego Comic-Con") as a favor to his friend John Lasseter.[^402] Miyazaki also expressed his opinion about [the terrorist attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlie_Hebdo_shooting "Charlie Hebdo shooting") at the offices of the French [satirical magazine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satirical_magazine "Satirical magazine") *[Charlie Hebdo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlie_Hebdo "Charlie Hebdo")*, criticizing the magazine's decision to publish the content cited as the catalyst for the incident; he felt caricatures should be made of politicians, not cultures.[^403] In November 2016, Miyazaki believed "many of the people who voted for [Brexit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brexit "Brexit") and [Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_2016_presidential_campaign "Donald Trump 2016 presidential campaign") " were affected by the increase in unemployment due to companies "building cars in Mexico because of low wages and \[selling\] them in the US".[^404] He did not think Donald Trump would be [elected president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election "2016 United States presidential election"), calling it "a terrible thing", but said Trump's political opponent [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton "Hillary Clinton") was "terrible as well".[^404]

## Themes

Miyazaki's works are characterized by the recurrence of themes such as feminism,[^405] [^406] [^407] [environmentalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmentalism "Environmentalism"), [pacifism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacifism "Pacifism"),[^408] [^409] [^410] love, and family.[^411] [^412] [^413] His narratives are also notable for not pitting a hero against an unsympathetic antagonist;[^414] [^415] [^416] Miyazaki felt *Spirited Away* ' s Chihiro "manages not because she has destroyed the 'evil', but because she has acquired the ability to survive".[^417]

Miyazaki's films often emphasize environmentalism and the Earth's fragility.[^418] [Margaret Talbot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaret_Talbot "Margaret Talbot") stated Miyazaki dislikes modern technology, and believes much of modern culture is "thin and shallow and fake"; he anticipates a time with "no more high-rises".[^419] Miyazaki felt frustrated growing up in the [Shōwa period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sh%C5%8Dwa_period "Shōwa period") from 1955 to 1965 because "nature—the mountains and rivers—was being destroyed in the name of economic progress".[^420] Peter Schellhase of *[The Imaginative Conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Imaginative_Conservative "The Imaginative Conservative")* identified that several antagonists of Miyazaki's films "attempt to dominate nature in pursuit of political domination, and are ultimately destructive to both nature and human civilization".[^413] Miyazaki is critical of exploitation under both communism and [capitalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitalism "Capitalism"), as well as [globalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globalization "Globalization") and its effects on modern life, believing "a company is common property of the people that work there".[^421] Ram Prakash Dwivedi identified values of [Mahatma Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahatma_Gandhi "Mahatma Gandhi") in the films of Miyazaki.[^422]

Several of Miyazaki's films feature anti-war themes. Daisuke Akimoto of *Animation Studies* categorized *Porco Rosso* as "anti-war propaganda" and felt the protagonist, Porco, transforms into a pig partly due to his extreme distaste of militarism.[^13] Akimoto also argues that *The Wind Rises* reflects Miyazaki's "antiwar pacifism", despite Miyazaki stating that the film does not attempt to "denounce" war.[^423] Schellhase also identifies *Princess Mononoke* as a pacifist film due to the protagonist, Ashitaka; instead of joining the campaign of revenge against humankind, as his ethnic history would lead him to do, Ashitaka strives for peace.[^413] David Loy and Linda Goodhew argue both *Nausicaä of the Valley of the Wind* and *Princess Mononoke* do not depict traditional evil, but the [Buddhist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist "Buddhist") roots of evil: greed, ill will, and delusion; according to Buddhism, the roots of evil must transform into "generosity, loving-kindness and wisdom" in order to overcome suffering, and both Nausicaä and Ashitaka accomplish this.[^424] When characters in Miyazaki's films are forced to engage in violence, it is shown as being a difficult task; in *Howl's Moving Castle*, Howl is forced to fight an inescapable battle in defense of those he loves, and it almost destroys him, though he is ultimately saved by Sophie's love and bravery.[^413]

Suzuki described Miyazaki as a feminist in reference to his attitude to female workers.[^425] Miyazaki has described his female characters as "brave, self-sufficient girls that don't think twice about fighting for what they believe in with all their heart", stating they may "need a friend, or a supporter, but never a saviour" and "any woman is just as capable of being a hero as any man".[^426] *Nausicaä of the Valley of the Wind* was lauded for its positive portrayal of women, particularly protagonist Nausicaä.[^159] [^161] Schellhase noted the female characters in Miyazaki's films are not objectified or sexualized, and possess complex and individual characteristics absent from Hollywood productions.[^413] Schellhase also identified a "coming of age" element for the heroines in Miyazaki's films, as they each discover "individual personality and strengths".[^413] Gabrielle Bellot of *[The Atlantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Atlantic "The Atlantic")* wrote that, in his films, Miyazaki "shows a keen understanding of the complexities of what it might mean to be a woman". In particular, Bellot cites *Nausicaä of the Valley of the Wind*, praising the film's challenging of gender expectations, and the strong and independent nature of Nausicaä. Bellot also noted *Princess Mononoke* ' s San represents the "conflict between selfhood and expression".[^427]

Miyazaki is concerned with the sense of wonder in young people, seeking to maintain themes of love and family in his films.[^413] Michael Toscano of *Curator* found Miyazaki "fears Japanese children are dimmed by a culture of overconsumption, overprotection, utilitarian education, careerism, techno-industrialism, and a secularism that is swallowing Japan's native animism".[^428] Schellhase wrote that several of Miyazaki's works feature themes of love and romance, but felt emphasis is placed on "the way lonely and vulnerable individuals are integrated into relationships of mutual reliance and responsibility, which generally benefit everyone around them".[^413] He also found many of the protagonists in Miyazaki's films present an idealized image of families, whereas others are dysfunctional.[^413]

Miyazaki forgoes traditional screenplays in his productions, instead developing the narrative as he designs the storyboards, stating "We never know where the story will go but we just keep working on the film as it develops".[^429] Miyazaki has employed traditional animation methods in all of his films, drawing each frame by hand; computer-generated imagery has been employed in several of his later films, beginning with *Princess Mononoke*, to "enrich the visual look",[^430] though he ensures each film can "retain the right ratio between working by hand and computer... and still be able to call my films 2D".[^431] He oversees every frame of his films.[^432] For character designs, Miyazaki draws original drafts used by animation directors to create reference sheets, which Miyazaki then corrects in his style.[^433] He prioritizes logical plots even for child-friendly contents.[^434]

Miyazaki has cited several Japanese artists as his influences, including [Sanpei Shirato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanpei_Shirato "Sanpei Shirato"),[^39] Osamu Tezuka, Soji Yamakawa,[^41] and Isao Takahata,[^435] and Western artists and animators like [Frédéric Back](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fr%C3%A9d%C3%A9ric_Back "Frédéric Back"),[^429] Jean Giraud,[^139] Paul Grimault,[^429] [Yuri Norstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuri_Norstein "Yuri Norstein"),[^436] and animation studio [Aardman Animations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aardman_Animations "Aardman Animations") (specifically the works of [Nick Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nick_Park "Nick Park")).[^437] [^14] Several authors have also influenced his works, including [Lewis Carroll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lewis_Carroll "Lewis Carroll"),[^431] [Roald Dahl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roald_Dahl "Roald Dahl"),[^439] Ursula K. Le Guin,[^146] [Philippa Pearce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippa_Pearce "Philippa Pearce"), [Rosemary Sutcliff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosemary_Sutcliff "Rosemary Sutcliff"), and [Antoine de Saint-Exupéry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antoine_de_Saint-Exup%C3%A9ry "Antoine de Saint-Exupéry"),[^49] Specific works that have influenced Miyazaki include *[Animal Farm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_Farm "Animal Farm")* (1945),[^431] *The Snow Queen* (1957),[^429] and *[The King and the Mockingbird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_King_and_the_Mockingbird "The King and the Mockingbird")* (1980);[^431] *The Snow Queen* is said to be the true catalyst for Miyazaki's filmography, influencing his training and work.[^440] When animating young children, Miyazaki often takes inspiration from his friends' children and memories of his own childhood.[^441]

## Personal life

![[~/×/b7570a8ec2151c7a8062b6b252fea137_MD5.jpg]]

Miyazaki's son, director Goro Miyazaki

Miyazaki's wife, Akemi Ōta (大田朱美), was born in 1938 and hired as an inbetween artist at Toei Animation in 1958, working on *Panda and the Magic Serpent* and *[Alakazam the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alakazam_the_Great "Alakazam the Great")* (1960).[^442] She and Miyazaki met at Toei in 1964,[^442] [^18] and they married in October 1965.[^52] At Toei, they worked together on *The Great Adventure of Horus, Prince of the Sun* and *The Wonderful World of Puss 'n Boots*.[^442] They have two sons: Goro, born in January 1967, and Keisuke, born in April 1969.[^69] Becoming a father changed Miyazaki and he tried to produce work to please his children.[^68]

Miyazaki initially fulfilled a promise to his wife that they would both continue to work after Goro's birth, dropping him off at [preschool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preschool "Preschool") for the day; however, upon seeing Goro's exhaustion walking home one day, Miyazaki decided they could not continue, and his wife quit in 1972 to stay at home and raise their children.[^443] [^442] She was reluctant to do so but considered it necessary to allow Miyazaki to focus on his work.[^442] Miyazaki's dedication to his work harmed his relationship with his children as he was often absent. Goro watched his father's works to "understand" him since the two rarely talked.[^444] Miyazaki said he "tried to be a good father, but in the end \[he\] wasn't a very good parent",[^443] and later said he felt he owed "that little boy an apology".[^27] In 2006, Goro said his father "gets zero marks as a father, but full marks as a director of animated films".[^444] [^15]

Goro worked at a landscape design firm before beginning to work at the Ghibli Museum;[^18] [^443] he designed the garden on its rooftop and eventually became its curator.[^18] [^68] Keisuke studied forestry at [Shinshu University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shinshu_University "Shinshu University") and works as a wood artist;[^18] [^443] [^445] he designed a woodcut print that appears in *Whisper of the Heart*.[^445] Miyazaki's niece, Mei Okuyama, who was the inspiration behind the character Mei in *My Neighbor Totoro*, is married to animation artist [Daisuke Tsutsumi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daisuke_Tsutsumi "Daisuke Tsutsumi").[^446]

## Legacy

![[~/×/cfeec9b3a49383cd726060c02dd3fee7_MD5.jpg]]

Wall mural depicting several anime characters

![[~/×/a3d27bee2c08e3654ec2c1bde84d29a1_MD5.jpg]]

Wall mural depicting several anime characters

Murals honoring Miyazaki's works in [Aguascalientes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aguascalientes "Aguascalientes"), Mexico (top), and [Limoges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limoges "Limoges"), France (bottom)

Miyazaki was described as the "godfather of animation in Japan" by [BBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC "BBC") 's Tessa Wong in 2016, citing his craftsmanship and humanity, the themes of his films, and his inspiration to younger artists.[^447] Courtney Lanning of *[Arkansas Democrat-Gazette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arkansas_Democrat-Gazette "Arkansas Democrat-Gazette")* named him one of the world's greatest animators, comparing him to Osamu Tezuka and [Walt Disney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Disney "Walt Disney");[^448] Miyazaki has been called "the Disney of Japan", though Helen McCarthy considered comparison to [Akira Kurosawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akira_Kurosawa "Akira Kurosawa") more appropriate due to the combination of grandeur and sensitivity in his work, dubbing him "the Kurosawa of animation".[^449]

Swapnil Dhruv Bose of *Far Out Magazine* wrote that Miyazaki's work "has shaped not only the future of animation but also filmmaking in general", and that it helped "generation after generation of young viewers to observe the magic that exists in the mundane".[^450] Richard James Havis of *[South China Morning Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_China_Morning_Post "South China Morning Post")* called him a "genius... who sets exacting standards for himself, his peers and studio staff".[^451] *[Paste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paste_\(magazine\) "Paste (magazine)")* ' s Toussaint Egan described Miyazaki as "one of anime's great auteurs", whose "stories of such singular thematic vision and unmistakable aesthetic" captured viewers otherwise unfamiliar with anime.[^452] Miyazaki became the subject of an exhibit at the [Academy Museum of Motion Pictures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Museum_of_Motion_Pictures "Academy Museum of Motion Pictures") in Los Angeles in 2021, featuring over 400 objects from his films.[^453]

Miyazaki has frequently been cited as an inspiration to numerous animators, directors and writers around the world, including [Wes Anderson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wes_Anderson "Wes Anderson"),[^454] [Tony Bancroft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Bancroft "Tony Bancroft"),[^254] [James Cameron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Cameron "James Cameron"),[^455] [Barry Cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barry_Cook "Barry Cook"),[^254] [Dean DeBlois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dean_DeBlois "Dean DeBlois"),[^456] [Guillermo del Toro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guillermo_del_Toro "Guillermo del Toro"),[^457] [Pete Docter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pete_Docter "Pete Docter"),[^458] Mamoru Hosoda,[^459] [Bong Joon Ho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bong_Joon_Ho "Bong Joon Ho"),[^460] [Travis Knight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Travis_Knight "Travis Knight"),[^461] John Lasseter,[^462] Nick Park,[^463] [Henry Selick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Selick "Henry Selick"),[^464] [Makoto Shinkai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makoto_Shinkai "Makoto Shinkai"),[^465] [Steven Spielberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steven_Spielberg "Steven Spielberg"),[^466] and [Gints Zilbalodis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gints_Zilbalodis "Gints Zilbalodis").[^467] [Glen Keane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glen_Keane "Glen Keane") said Miyazaki is a "huge influence" on [Walt Disney Animation Studios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Disney_Animation_Studios "Walt Disney Animation Studios") and has been "part of our heritage" ever since *[The Rescuers Down Under](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Rescuers_Down_Under "The Rescuers Down Under")* (1990).[^468] The [Disney Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disney_Renaissance "Disney Renaissance") era was also prompted by competition with the development of Miyazaki's films.[^469] Artists from [Pixar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pixar "Pixar") and [Aardman Studios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aardman_Studios "Aardman Studios") signed a tribute stating, "You're our inspiration, Miyazaki-san!" [^463] He has also been cited as inspiration for video game designers including [Shigeru Miyamoto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shigeru_Miyamoto "Shigeru Miyamoto") on *[The Legend of Zelda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Legend_of_Zelda "The Legend of Zelda")* [^470] and [Hironobu Sakaguchi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hironobu_Sakaguchi "Hironobu Sakaguchi") on *[Final Fantasy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Final_Fantasy "Final Fantasy")*,[^471] as well as the television series *[Avatar: The Last Airbender](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avatar:_The_Last_Airbender "Avatar: The Last Airbender")*,[^472] and the video game *[Ori and the Blind Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ori_and_the_Blind_Forest "Ori and the Blind Forest")* (2015).[^473]

[Several books have been written](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hayao_Miyazaki_bibliography "Hayao Miyazaki bibliography") about Miyazaki by scholars such as [Raz Greenberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raz_Greenberg "Raz Greenberg"), Helen McCarthy, and [Susan J. Napier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susan_J._Napier "Susan J. Napier");[^474] according to Jeff Lenburg, more papers have been written about Miyazaki than any other Japanese artist.[^475] Studio Ghibli has searched for some time for Miyazaki and Suzuki's successor to lead the studio;[^476] Kondō, the director of *Whisper of the Heart*, was initially considered, but died from a sudden heart attack in 1998.[^477] Some candidates were considered by 2023—including Miyazaki's son Goro, who declined—but the studio was not able to find a successor.[^478]

## Selected filmography

- *[The Castle of Cagliostro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Castle_of_Cagliostro "The Castle of Cagliostro")* (1979)
- *[Nausicaä of the Valley of the Wind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nausica%C3%A4_of_the_Valley_of_the_Wind_\(film\) "Nausicaä of the Valley of the Wind (film)")* (1984)
- *[Laputa: Castle in the Sky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laputa:_Castle_in_the_Sky "Laputa: Castle in the Sky")* (1986)
- *[My Neighbor Totoro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/My_Neighbor_Totoro "My Neighbor Totoro")* (1988)
- *[Kiki's Delivery Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiki%27s_Delivery_Service "Kiki's Delivery Service")* (1989)
- *[Porco Rosso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porco_Rosso "Porco Rosso")* (1992)
- *[Princess Mononoke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princess_Mononoke "Princess Mononoke")* (1997)
- *[Spirited Away](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spirited_Away "Spirited Away")* (2001)
- *[Howl's Moving Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howl%27s_Moving_Castle_\(film\) "Howl's Moving Castle (film)")* (2004)
- *[Ponyo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ponyo "Ponyo")* (2008)
- *[The Wind Rises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Wind_Rises "The Wind Rises")* (2013)
- *[The Boy and the Heron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Boy_and_the_Heron "The Boy and the Heron")* (2023)

Miyazaki won the [Ōfuji Noburō Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%8Cfuji_Nobur%C5%8D_Award "Ōfuji Noburō Award") at the [Mainichi Film Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainichi_Film_Awards "Mainichi Film Awards") for *The Castle of Cagliostro* (1979), *Nausicaä of the Valley of the Wind* (1984), *Laputa: Castle in the Sky* (1986),[^479] *My Neighbor Totoro* (1988),[^480] and *The Boy and the Heron* (2023),[^481] and the [Mainichi Film Award for Best Animation Film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainichi_Film_Award_for_Best_Animation_Film "Mainichi Film Award for Best Animation Film") for *Kiki's Delivery Service* (1989),[^480] *Porco Rosso* (1992),[^482] *Princess Mononoke* (1997), *Spirited Away* (2001),[^480] and *Whale Hunt* (2001).[^482] *Spirited Away* and *The Boy and the Heron* were awarded the Academy Award for Best Animated Feature,[^286] [^378] while *Howl's Moving Castle* (2004) and *The Wind Rises* (2013) received nominations.[^314] [^360] He was named a [Person of Cultural Merit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Person_of_Cultural_Merit "Person of Cultural Merit") by the Japanese government in November 2012, for outstanding cultural contributions.[^483] *[Time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_\(magazine\) "Time (magazine)")* named him one of the [100 most influential people in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_100 "Time 100") in 2005 and 2024,[^318] [^484] and [Gold House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gold_House "Gold House") honored him on its [Most Impactful Asians A100 list](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gold_House#A100 "Gold House") in 2024.[^485] He was an honoree of the [Ramon Magsaysay Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramon_Magsaysay_Award "Ramon Magsaysay Award") in 2024 for his usage of art to "illuminate the human condition".[^486] Miyazaki's other accolades include several [Annie Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annie_Awards "Annie Awards"),[^487] [^488] [^489] [Japan Academy Film Prizes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan_Academy_Film_Prize "Japan Academy Film Prize"),[^490] [^491] [^359] [Kinema Junpo Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kinema_Junpo_Awards "Kinema Junpo Awards"),[^492] [^493] and [Tokyo Anime Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokyo_Anime_Awards "Tokyo Anime Awards").[^348] [^494] [^495]
